Well, I came because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. Yes… yes, I am. I mean, I used to be the one people counted on for things. At the senior center, I was supposed to lead this big event — it had a lot of moving pieces, sign-ups and calls and, oh, the decorations committee was asking me about tablecloths, and I remember thinking I’d written it down somewhere, but then I couldn’t find the paper.

And at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it in a mean way, but I know why she’s doing it. She doesn’t trust that I’ll remember or keep it organized on my own anymore. That’s been very upsetting.

I mean, I used to be the one people counted on for things. At the senior center, I was supposed to lead this big event — it had a lot of moving pieces, sign-ups and calls and, oh, the decorations committee was asking me about tablecloths, and I remember thinking I’d written it down somewhere, but then I couldn’t find the paper.\n\nAnyway… I had to give it up. I just couldn’t keep it all straight, and that was hard. Embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it in a mean way, but I know why she’s doing it. She doesn’t trust that I’ll remember or keep it organized on my own anymore. That’s been very upsetting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes… it has. I keep thinking back, and it really became this troublesome about… oh, a year and a few months ago. Maybe a year and three months, something like that.

It was around when my husband started needing regular help with all his appointments and the daily arrangements. There were suddenly so many papers and calls and times to keep straight, and I used to think, well, I’ve managed a household all these years, I can manage this too. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.

Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. I just couldn’t keep it all straight, and that was hard. Embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it in a mean way, but I know why she’s doing it. She doesn’t trust that I’ll remember or keep it organized on my own anymore. That’s been very upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it learning new things exactly, but I do forget things I’m supposed to do. Like I’ll mean to return a call and then find the note days later. Or a bill will be sitting under a stack of mail and I had completely forgotten it was there.\n\nAnd my daughter will ask, “Did you pick up that thing we talked about?” and I just feel awful because I’d promised her I’d run that errand, and it went right out of my head. Oh, and with my husband’s appointments — more than once I realized too late that I never called to confirm one. Then I start thinking, well, where did I put the number, and I go looking through the kitchen pile again… and then I get sidetracked by something else in the pile. It’s very frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to remember and keep track of things has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking back, and it really became this troublesome about… oh, a year and a few months ago. Maybe a year and three months, something like that.\n\nIt was around when my husband started needing regular help with all his appointments and the daily arrangements. There were suddenly so many papers and calls and times to keep straight, and I used to think, well, I’ve managed a household all these years, I can manage this too. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I keep thinking back, and it really became this troublesome about… oh, a year and a few months ago. There were suddenly so many papers and calls and times to keep straight, and I used to think, well, I’ve managed a household all these years, I can manage this too. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.

But the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.

The point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.

effort.\n  manifestation: When there’s a form to fill out, insurance papers to read, or a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off because my head feels worn out before I even start.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m forever hunting for things I just had in my hand, like the car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, recipes for the church supper, or the folder I need to take to the doctor’s office.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: If the television is on in the next room or two people start talking near me at the senior center, my mind just follows that instead, and then I lose my place in what I was writing or planning.\n- key: S009\n  name: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\n  description: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve missed returning calls, left bills sitting under a stack of mail, forgotten errands I promised my daughter I’d do, and more than once realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: Even when I’m trying to sit politely in a meeting, I catch myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around so much that people look over at me.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: During volunteer meetings or even at supper, I keep getting up to check the calendar, look for a paper, refill water, or just move around, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated and listen.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even as a little girl, my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, forgot books and homework, and needed reminders to finish seatwork before I could go outside.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I had to give up leading a big senior-center event, and my daughter has started double-checking bills and appointments because she no longer trusts that I can keep everything straight on my own.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: It has been causing this much trouble for about a year and three months, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and daily arrangements.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.

Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. It’s not just misplacing my glasses or forgetting why I walked into a room — although, goodness, I do that too, and then I find myself standing in the kitchen staring at the kettle.

hands, tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around so much that people look over at me.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: During volunteer meetings or even at supper, I keep getting up to check the calendar, look for a paper, refill water, or just move around, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated and listen.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even as a little girl, my teachers description: required\n  manifestation: I had to give up leading a big senior-center event, and my daughter has started double-checking bills and appointments because she no longer trusts that I can keep everything straight on my own.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: It has been causing this much trouble for about a year and three months, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and daily arrangements.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. It’s not just misplacing my glasses or forgetting why I walked into a room — although, goodness, I do that too, and then I find myself standing in the kitchen staring at the kettle.\n\nBut the bigger thing is I had to give up leading that big event at the senior center. I used to be able to handle all the little pieces, and now I just can’t keep them in order. And my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’s sweet about it, but it makes me feel like she doesn’t trust me to manage it on my own anymore.\n\nSo yes… it’s interfering with my life. Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. I mean, it’s worse now, or maybe I notice it more because there’s more to keep track of, but even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless.” I remember that phrase because my mother did not like it — she’d read those report cards at the kitchen table, and we had this little yellow table, funny the things you remember.

I used to be able to handle all the little pieces, and now I just can’t keep them in order. And my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’s sweet about it, but it makes me feel like she doesn’t trust me to manage it on my own anymore.\n\nSo yes… it’s interfering with my life. More than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these problems with organization, memory, or restlessness for most of your life, or did they start more recently as you've described?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. I mean, it’s worse now, or maybe I notice it more because there’s more to keep track of, but even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless.” I remember that phrase because my mother did not like it — she’d read those report cards at the kitchen table, and we had this little yellow table, funny the things you remember.\n\nThey’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… overwhelmed is exactly the word. I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore.

My kitchen table is covered right now with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists I keep making — I’ll start one list, then find another envelope and write on that, and then I don’t know which one is the “real” list. And I sit there looking at all of it, trying to decide what to do first, and somehow the whole day just slips away.

It makes me anxious, yes. Not like panic, exactly, but that awful tight feeling of knowing there are things I’m supposed to handle and I can’t get them lined up in my head.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. I sit down at the kitchen table and it’s just… papers everywhere — my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, these little lists I start and don’t finish. And then I look at it all and I don’t know what to do first.\n\nI meant to clear it off yesterday, but then I saw a church notice and remembered I hadn’t called someone back, and then there was another paper under that, and before I know it the whole day is gone. It sounds silly, maybe, but it’s gotten really upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities at home or in other areas of your life because of this disorganization?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I am. I mean, I used to be the one people counted on for things. At the senior center, I was supposed to lead this big event — it had a lot of moving pieces, sign-ups and calls and, oh, the decorations committee was asking me about tablecloths, and I remember thinking I’d written it down somewhere, but then I couldn’t find the paper.\n\nAnyway… I had to give it up. I just couldn’t keep it all straight, and that was hard. Embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it in a mean way, but I know why she’s doing it. She doesn’t trust that I’ll remember or keep it organized on my own anymore. That’s been very upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it learning new things exactly, but I do forget things I’m supposed to do. Like I’ll mean to return a call and then find the note days later. Or a bill will be sitting under a stack of mail and I had completely forgotten it was there.\n\nAnd my daughter will ask, “Did you pick up that thing we talked about?” and I just feel awful because I’d promised her I’d run that errand, and it went right out of my head. Oh, and with my husband’s appointments — more than once I realized too late that I never called to confirm one. Then I start thinking, well, where did I put the number, and I go looking through the kitchen pile again… and then I get sidetracked by something else in the pile. It’s very frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to remember and keep track of things has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking back, and it really became this troublesome about… oh, a year and a few months ago. Maybe a year and three months, something like that.\n\nIt was around when my husband started needing regular help with all his appointments and the daily arrangements. There were suddenly so many papers and calls and times to keep straight, and I used to think, well, I’ve managed a household all these years, I can manage this too. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. I think it’s more that I lose my attention first, and then it looks like forgetting. Like, if the television is on in the next room — even if I’m not watching it — my mind sort of goes over there. Then when I look back at my paper, I’ve lost my place completely. And I’ll think, “Now what was I doing?” It feels like I forgot, but really I think I got pulled away. Does that make sense?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. I sit down at the kitchen table and it’s just… papers everywhere — my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, these little lists I start and don’t finish. And then I look at it all and I don’t know what to do first.\n\nI meant to clear it off yesterday, but then I saw a church notice and remembered I hadn’t called someone back, and then there was another paper under that, and before I know it the whole day is gone. It sounds silly, maybe, but it’s gotten really upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities at home or in other areas of your life because of this disorganization?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I am. I mean, I used to be the one people counted on for things. At the senior center, I was supposed to lead this big event — it had a lot of moving pieces, sign-ups and calls and, oh, the decorations committee was asking me about tablecloths, and I remember thinking I’d written it down somewhere, but then I couldn’t find the paper.\n\nAnyway… I had to give it up. I just couldn’t keep it all straight, and that was hard. Embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it in a mean way, but I know why she’s doing it. She doesn’t trust that I’ll remember or keep it organized on my own anymore. That’s been very upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it learning new things exactly, but I do forget things I’m supposed to do. Like I’ll mean to return a call and then find the note days later. Or a bill will be sitting under a stack of mail and I had completely forgotten it was there.\n\nAnd my daughter will ask, “Did you pick up that thing we talked about?” and I just feel awful because I’d promised her I’d run that errand, and it went right out of my head. Oh, and with my husband’s appointments — more than once I realized too late that I never called to confirm one. Then I start thinking, well, where did I put the number, and I go looking through the kitchen pile again… and then I get sidetracked by something else in the pile. It’s very frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to remember and keep track of things has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking back, and it really became this troublesome about… oh, a year and a few months ago. Maybe a year and three months, something like that.\n\nIt was around when my husband started needing regular help with all his appointments and the daily arrangements. There were suddenly so many papers and calls and times to keep straight, and I used to think, well, I’ve managed a household all these years, I can manage this too. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. More than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these problems with organization, memory, or restlessness for most of your life, or did they start more recently as you've described?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. It’s not just misplacing my glasses or forgetting why I walked into a room — although, goodness, I do that too, and then I find myself standing in the kitchen staring at the kettle.\n\nBut the bigger thing is I had to give up leading that big event at the senior center. I used to be able to handle all the little pieces, and now I just can’t keep them in order. And my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’s sweet about it, but it makes me feel like she doesn’t trust me to manage it on my own anymore.\n\nSo yes… it’s interfering with my life. More than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these problems with organization, memory, or restlessness for most of your life, or did they start more recently as you've described?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. I mean, it’s worse now, or maybe I notice it more because there’s more to keep track of, but even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless.” I remember that phrase because my mother did not like it — she’d read those report cards at the kitchen table, and we had this little yellow table, funny the things you remember.\n\nThey’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So… yes, I suppose it’s been there most of my life, in some form.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to keep things organized or remember appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is exactly the word. I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore.\n\nMy kitchen table is covered right now with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists I keep making — I’ll start one list, then find another envelope and write on that, and then I don’t know which one is the “real” list. And I sit there looking at all of it, trying to decide what to do first, and somehow the whole day just slips away.\n\nIt makes me anxious, yes. Not like panic, exactly, but that awful tight feeling of knowing there are things I’m supposed to handle and I can’t get them lined up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and memory are more related to having trouble paying attention or to actually forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I lose my attention first, and then it looks like forgetting. Like, if the television is on in the next room — even if I’m not watching it — my mind sort of goes over there. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me while I’m trying to write something down or plan something, I just… follow their conversation without meaning to.\n\nThen when I look back at my paper, I’ve lost my place completely. And I’ll think, “Now what was I doing?” It feels like I forgot, but really I think I got pulled away. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so… if I could keep my mind on the thing long enough to get it written down properly, I’d probably do better.

But what happens is, say I’m trying to make a note, and the television is on in the next room — even if it’s something silly, like a cooking show or the news weather, my mind just goes right to it. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back at my paper and I’ve lost where I was, and sometimes I don’t even finish writing the thought down.

So yes… I don’t know that it’s the remembering exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away before I can keep track of it.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to remember things better if you could just focus long enough to write them down or keep track of them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… if I could keep my mind on the thing long enough to get it written down properly, I’d probably do better.\n\nBut what happens is, say I’m trying to make a note, and the television is on in the next room — even if it’s something silly, like a cooking show or the news weather, my mind just goes right to it. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back at my paper and I’ve lost where I was, and sometimes I don’t even finish writing the thought down.\n\nSo yes… I don’t know that it’s the remembering exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away before I can keep track of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. I used to be able to handle all the little pieces, and now I just can’t keep them in order. And my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’s sweet about it, but it makes me feel like she doesn’t trust me to manage it on my own anymore.\n\nSo yes… it’s interfering with my life. More than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these problems with organization, memory, or restlessness for most of your life, or did they start more recently as you've described?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. I mean, it’s worse now, or maybe I notice it more because there’s more to keep track of, but even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless.” I remember that phrase because my mother did not like it — she’d read those report cards at the kitchen table, and we had this little yellow table, funny the things you remember.\n\nThey’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. I think it’s more paying attention in the first place. Because if the television is on in the next room, my mind just sort of follows it, even when I don’t want it to. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. It’s not just misplacing my glasses or forgetting why I walked into a room — although, goodness, I do that too, and then I find myself standing in the kitchen staring at the kettle.\n\nBut the bigger thing is I had to give up leading that big event at the senior center. I used to be able to handle all the little pieces, and now I just can’t keep them in order. And my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’s sweet about it, but it makes me feel like she doesn’t trust me to manage it on my own anymore.\n\nSo yes… it’s interfering with my life. More than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these problems with organization, memory, or restlessness for most of your life, or did they start more recently as you've described?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. I mean, it’s worse now, or maybe I notice it more because there’s more to keep track of, but even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless.” I remember that phrase because my mother did not like it — she’d read those report cards at the kitchen table, and we had this little yellow table, funny the things you remember.\n\nThey’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So… yes, I suppose it’s been there most of my life, in some form.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to keep things organized or remember appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is exactly the word. I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore.\n\nMy kitchen table is covered right now with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists I keep making — I’ll start one list, then find another envelope and write on that, and then I don’t know which one is the “real” list. And I sit there looking at all of it, trying to decide what to do first, and somehow the whole day just slips away.\n\nIt makes me anxious, yes. Not like panic, exactly, but that awful tight feeling of knowing there are things I’m supposed to handle and I can’t get them lined up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and memory are more related to having trouble paying attention or to actually forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I lose my attention first, and then it looks like forgetting. Like, if the television is on in the next room — even if I’m not watching it — my mind sort of goes over there. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me while I’m trying to write something down or plan something, I just… follow their conversation without meaning to.\n\nThen when I look back at my paper, I’ve lost my place completely. And I’ll think, “Now what was I doing?” It feels like I forgot, but really I think I got pulled away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to remember things better if you could just focus long enough to write them down or keep track of them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… if I could keep my mind on the thing long enough to get it written down properly, I’d probably do better.\n\nBut what happens is, say I’m trying to make a note, and the television is on in the next room — even if it’s something silly, like a cooking show or the news weather, my mind just goes right to it. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back at my paper and I’ve lost where I was, and sometimes I don’t even finish writing the thought down.\n\nSo yes… I don’t know that it’s the remembering exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away before I can keep track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't remember things or that you have trouble paying attention to them in the first place?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention in the first place. Because if the television is on in the next room, my mind just sort of follows it, even when I don’t want it to. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me — and they might just be talking about someone’s grandchild or the lunch menu, you know — I’m suddenly listening to them instead of what I was writing.\n\nThen I look back down and I’ve lost my place. I don’t remember what I was planning or where I was on the page. So it looks like memory, but I think I get pulled away first.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me — and they might just be talking about someone’s grandchild or the lunch menu, you know — I’m suddenly listening to them instead of what I was writing.\n\nThen I look back down and I’ve lost my place. I don’t remember what I was planning or where I was on the page. So it looks like memory, but I think I get pulled away first.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your difficulties with organization and memory would improve if you were in a quieter, less distracting environment?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"If the television is on in the next room or two people start talking near me at the senior center, my mind just follows that instead, and then I lose my place in what I was writing or planning.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do think a quieter place would help. I mean, if there isn’t anything pulling at me, I can stay with what I’m doing a little better.

It’s the outside things that get me. Like if the television is on in the next room, even if I don’t care about what’s on, my mind just follows it. Or at the senior center — oh, that place can get so chatty, especially around lunchtime — if two people start talking near me, I’ll suddenly be listening to them instead of what I was trying to write down or plan.

Then I look back and I’ve lost my place completely. So yes… I think less noise and fewer distractions would probably make it easier for me.

It sounds silly, maybe, but it’s gotten really upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities at home or in other areas of your life because of this disorganization?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I am. I mean, I used to be the one people counted on for things. At the senior center, I was supposed to lead this big event — it had a lot of moving pieces, sign-ups and calls and, oh, the decorations committee was asking me about tablecloths, and I remember thinking I’d written it down somewhere, but then I couldn’t find the paper.\n\nAnyway… I had to give it up. I just couldn’t keep it all straight, and that was hard. Embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it in a mean way, but I know why she’s doing it. She doesn’t trust that I’ll remember or keep it organized on my own anymore. That’s been very upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it learning new things exactly, but I do forget things I’m supposed to do. Like I’ll mean to return a call and then find the note days later. It’s very frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to remember and keep track of things has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking back, and it really became this troublesome about… oh, a year and a few months ago. Maybe a year and three months, something like that.\n\nIt was around when my husband started needing regular help with all his appointments and the daily arrangements. There were suddenly so many papers and calls and times to keep straight, and I used to think, well, I’ve managed a household all these years, I can manage this too. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. It’s not just misplacing my glasses or forgetting why I walked into a room — although, goodness, I do that too, and then I find myself standing in the kitchen staring at the kettle.\n\nBut the bigger thing is I had to give up leading that big event at the senior center. I used to be able to handle all the little pieces, and now I just can’t keep them in order. And my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’s sweet about it, but it makes me feel like she doesn’t trust me to manage it on my own anymore.\n\nSo yes… it’s interfering with my life. More than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these problems with organization, memory, or restlessness for most of your life, or did they start more recently as you've described?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. I mean, it’s worse now, or maybe I notice it more because there’s more to keep track of, but even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless.” I remember that phrase because my mother did not like it — she’d read those report cards at the kitchen table, and we had this little yellow table, funny the things you remember.\n\nThey’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So… yes, I suppose it’s been there most of my life, in some form.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to keep things organized or remember appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is exactly the word. I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore.\n\nMy kitchen table is covered right now with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists I keep making — I’ll start one list, then find another envelope and write on that, and then I don’t know which one is the “real” list. Not like panic, exactly, but that awful tight feeling of knowing there are things I’m supposed to handle and I can’t get them lined up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and memory are more related to having trouble paying attention or to actually forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I lose my attention first, and then it looks like forgetting. effort.\n  manifestation: When there’s a form to fill out, insurance papers to read, or a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off because my head feels worn out Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: If the television is on in the next room or two people start talking near me at the senior center, my mind just follows that instead, and then I lose my place in what I was writing or planning.\n- key: S009\n  name: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\n  description: Often I don’t remember what I was planning or where I was on the page. So it looks like memory, but I think I get pulled away first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and memory would improve if you were in a quieter, less distracting environment?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think a quieter place would help. I mean, if there isn’t anything pulling at me, I can stay with what I’m doing a little better.\n\nIt’s the outside things that get me. Like if the television is on in the next room, even if I don’t care about what’s on, my mind just follows it. Or at the senior center — oh, that place can get so chatty, especially around lunchtime — if two people start talking near me, I’ll suddenly be listening to them instead of what I was trying to write down or plan.\n\nThen I look back and I’ve lost my place completely. So yes… I think less noise and fewer distractions would probably make it easier for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your difficulties with organization and memory would be better explained by a problem with your attention or by a decline in your cognitive abilities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more attention, honestly. I mean, I don’t feel like I’m just losing my mind or anything like that… though at my age, you do start wondering, and then my neighbor was telling me about her cousin who— well, anyway.

What happens to me is, if the television is on in the next room, my mind just goes right to it. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me, I start listening even when I’m trying not to. Then when I look back at what I was writing down or trying to plan, I’ve lost my place.

So I’d say it’s more that I get pulled off track before I can keep hold of what I was doing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. I sit down at the kitchen table and it’s just… papers everywhere — my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, these little lists I start and don’t finish. And then I look at it all and I don’t know what to do first.\n\nI meant to clear it off yesterday, but then I saw a church notice and remembered I hadn’t called someone back, and then there was another paper under that, and before I know it the whole day is gone. It sounds silly, maybe, but it’s gotten really upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting responsibilities at home or in other areas of your life because of this disorganization?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I am. I mean, I used to be the one people counted on for things. At the senior center, I was supposed to lead this big event — it had a lot of moving pieces, sign-ups and calls and, oh, the decorations committee was asking me about tablecloths, and I remember thinking I’d written it down somewhere, but then I couldn’t find the paper.\n\nAnyway… I had to give it up. I just couldn’t keep it all straight, and that was hard. Embarrassing, really.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it in a mean way, but I know why she’s doing it. She doesn’t trust that I’ll remember or keep it organized on my own anymore. That’s been very upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it learning new things exactly, but I do forget things I’m supposed to do. Like I’ll mean to return a call and then find the note days later. Or a bill will be sitting under a stack of mail and I had completely forgotten it was there.\n\nAnd my daughter will ask, “Did you pick up that thing we talked about?” and I just feel awful because I’d promised her I’d run that errand, and it went right out of my head. Oh, and with my husband’s appointments — more than once I realized too late that I never called to confirm one. Then I start thinking, well, where did I put the number, and I go looking through the kitchen pile again… and then I get sidetracked by something else in the pile. It’s very frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to remember and keep track of things has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking back, and it really became this troublesome about… oh, a year and a few months ago. Maybe a year and three months, something like that.\n\nIt was around when my husband started needing regular help with all his appointments and the daily arrangements. There were suddenly so many papers and calls and times to keep straight, and I used to think, well, I’ve managed a household all these years, I can manage this too. But that’s when I really noticed I wasn’t keeping track the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. If the television is on in the next room, even softly, I’ll start listening to that instead of what I’m supposed to be doing. Or at the senior center, if two people nearby start talking — and you know, there’s always somebody talking there — my mind just goes with their conversation.\n\nThen I look back down at what I was writing or planning and I’ve completely lost my place. I’ll sit there thinking, “Now what was I doing?” It’s frustrating, because I really am trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit nicely, like in a meeting, I’ll realize I’m rubbing my hands together or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen without even noticing, which is terrible because that sound drives people mad.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair too, and then someone looks over and I think, oh goodness, I’m doing it again. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude, I just… I can’t seem to sit completely still.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop doing any activities or responsibilities that you used to enjoy or handle because of your memory, organization, or restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, and I don’t like admitting that. I used to help lead this big event at the senior center — there were sign-up sheets and phone calls and making sure people brought things, all of that. I used to enjoy it, honestly. It made me feel useful.\n\nBut the last time, I just couldn’t keep it all straight. I’d put a paper somewhere and then not know where it went, or I’d think I’d called someone and then maybe I hadn’t. And you know, those events get so busy — somebody always wants the coffee set up a certain way, and then there’s the tablecloths, and I remember one year we had these awful folding chairs that pinched your fingers — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, I had to step back from leading it. And now my daughter has started checking behind me with the bills and appointments. She doesn’t say it cruelly, but I can tell she doesn’t trust me to keep everything straight by myself anymore. That’s been hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends because of your difficulties with organization, memory, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my daughter, I suppose. She’s kind about it, but she’s started double-checking my bills and appointments, because I think she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep it all straight on my own anymore. That stings a bit.\n\nAnd at the senior center, I had to stop leading that big event I used to help run. I always liked doing it — all the sign-up sheets and who’s bringing what, though honestly those potluck tables get so crowded and somebody always brings three bean salads — but anyway, I just couldn’t manage it reliably anymore. So yes, it has affected things. It makes me feel less capable, and I think other people notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and organizational problems are significantly interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is… more than I’d like to admit. It’s not just misplacing my glasses or forgetting why I walked into a room — although, goodness, I do that too, and then I find myself standing in the kitchen staring at the kettle.\n\nBut the bigger thing is I had to give up leading that big event at the senior center. I used to be able to handle all the little pieces, and now I just can’t keep them in order. And my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’s sweet about it, but it makes me feel like she doesn’t trust me to manage it on my own anymore.\n\nSo yes… it’s interfering with my life. More than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any of these problems with organization, memory, or restlessness for most of your life, or did they start more recently as you've described?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think it goes way back… not just recent. I mean, it’s worse now, or maybe I notice it more because there’s more to keep track of, but even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like, “bright but careless.” I remember that phrase because my mother did not like it — she’d read those report cards at the kitchen table, and we had this little yellow table, funny the things you remember.\n\nThey’d say I forgot my books, forgot homework, needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So… yes, I suppose it’s been there most of my life, in some form.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to keep things organized or remember appointments?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is exactly the word. I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore.\n\nMy kitchen table is covered right now with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists I keep making — I’ll start one list, then find another envelope and write on that, and then I don’t know which one is the “real” list. And I sit there looking at all of it, trying to decide what to do first, and somehow the whole day just slips away.\n\nIt makes me anxious, yes. Not like panic, exactly, but that awful tight feeling of knowing there are things I’m supposed to handle and I can’t get them lined up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and memory are more related to having trouble paying attention or to actually forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I lose my attention first, and then it looks like forgetting. Like, if the television is on in the next room — even if I’m not watching it — my mind sort of goes over there. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me while I’m trying to write something down or plan something, I just… follow their conversation without meaning to.\n\nThen when I look back at my paper, I’ve lost my place completely. And I’ll think, “Now what was I doing?” It feels like I forgot, but really I think I got pulled away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to remember things better if you could just focus long enough to write them down or keep track of them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… if I could keep my mind on the thing long enough to get it written down properly, I’d probably do better.\n\nBut what happens is, say I’m trying to make a note, and the television is on in the next room — even if it’s something silly, like a cooking show or the news weather, my mind just goes right to it. Or at the senior center, if two people start talking near me, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back at my paper and I’ve lost where I was, and sometimes I don’t even finish writing the thought down.\n\nSo yes… I don’t know that it’s the remembering exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away before I can keep track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't remember things or that you have trouble paying attention to them in the first place?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention in the first place.
